Subscription lifecycle experiment

2026-11-09 · actual production connection dispatch · isolated fixture processes

No production runtime candidate

The baseline does not establish a credible 20% whole-cycle gain from runtime lifecycle removal. Production execution remains unchanged. This is not an upper bound on runtime contention under concurrency, and no alternating A/B result is claimed.

Borrowing the server runtime fails the deterministic two-slot saturation check: outer pulls hold the workers needed by nested SQLite work. Independent current-thread reader runtimes pass the same saturation/progress fixture. That alternative removes async worker pools while retaining per-reader SQLite pools and snapshots; it remains a draft mechanism pending a worthwhile CPU target.

Ordinary runtime defaults: bounded pilot

One unprofiled fresh process per shape; 16-core defaults, at most 16 subscriptions. Separate profiles are excluded from these timings. The component probe constructs and joins 32 runtimes sequentially without SQL, including worker CPU.

SubscriptionsServer cycle CPU msPure runtime CPU ms/cycleActive server CPU ms / 10 sActive projections
127.741.37186.4310
164,980.681.25810,430.56160

At 16 subscriptions, admission/checkpoint used 4,874.90 ms server CPU, including 1,715.08 ms measured store-opening caller CPU. Five cohort cycles repeated 80 store opens and 160 projections. Ten unrelated writes caused 160 projections and no changed frames. Store-opening and projection counters omit worker CPU and are not a complete partition.

The 16-client stack profile contains schema parsing and SQLite allocation/mutex waits under Reader::step → open_read_only_store → SqliteStore::open → configure_write_connection → sqlite3ReadSchema. It also contains query compilation/execution and parked Tokio workers. Stack-sample counts include waiting threads, so they are not CPU percentages.

The sequential runtime probe does not isolate concurrent runtime/store interactions. Both server and reader concurrency differ from the capped matrix, so comparing those timings does not measure a runtime-sharing speedup. The unprofiled 16-client run also spent 371.14 ms CPU after cleanup despite zero SQL/projections and no counted stage activity. That tail remains unexplained.

All four default/profile samples balanced runtimes, worker threads, wake threads and SQLite opens/closes and restored warmed FDs. The default server retained idle blocking workers: 20 threads warm, 323 with 16 subscriptions, 50 drained. The one-client observer retired during its post-cleanup window. Ten capture receipts and 2,690 payload bytes survived in each unprofiled writer; recording API CPU was 92.99/91.69 ms, separate from server and child CPU.

Profiles and every raw sample remain under /tmp/subscription-experiment-20261109/. The next measure command and revised ownership prediction are in the linked experiment note. Broader acceptance remains with measure/gate.

Capped baseline

Server CPU milliseconds, median (range). Cycle includes admission, first reading, checkpoint and cleanup. Five fresh processes per shape, reversing shape and window order. Two async workers per runtime; four server blocking workers. These are deliberately capped measurements, not installed-default timings.

Runtime caller CPU excludes worker initialization/shutdown CPU. The separate default pilot's sequential runtime probe includes worker CPU through join. Lower thread counts alone are not a whole-cycle improvement.

What the fixture proves

Every sample serves 127 scoped Sessions from a database with 128 Sessions, 64 Tasks and 32 completed Graphs. A separate writer produces ten capture facts and ten attachment-valid activity updates on the unrelated Session. Actual title changes and process exits without completion writes reach every subscribed client. Each sample includes 32 and 128 single-subscription cycles, five cohort cycles, checkpoints, sibling reads and a 2.2-second post-cleanup window.

Reader runtimes, runtime threads and wake threads balance in the completed capped samples; post-cleanup SQL and projection counts are zero. The shared server blocking pool retains idle workers until shutdown. File descriptors return to the warmed baseline. Runtime and projection counters are test-only; SQLite VM counters reset after each statement execution.

Failures and scope

Ten focused subscription/cancellation/saturation tests passed. The separate queued-cancel acceptance failed: one runtime was constructed after queued setup was canceled, where zero was required. Cancellation during admitted setup/read retained sibling checkpoints and safely reused request IDs. The two-slot borrowed-runtime fixture confirmed nested Store work cannot run until outer pulls release the pool; independent current-thread readers progress.

Initial pilot readiness parsing and compile errors are retained under the receipt directory and excluded from timing samples. This experiment does not measure Task identifier resolution, Process projections, distinct Task demands, kernel wakeups, or all installed orchestration. Existing observer baselines and the original measure.html are unchanged.

Server CPU excludes the Python driver and writer. Writer API CPU excludes fixture validation. Phase child-CPU deltas exclude resource-inventory ps calls, which occur between phases; cumulative child totals also include setup/inventory and are not application child work. Default-pilot resources additionally expose inventory child CPU.
